F_0 = \frac{2.99/3}{2.5/5} = \frac{0.997}{0.5} = 1.99 < F_{0.05,3,5} = 5.41 \quad (P = 0.23) .
\]
There is no longer evidence of lack of fit. $R^2$ has risen from $0.749$ to $0.909$, with the
same number of parameters, and $MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 5.49/8 = 0.69$ is now close to the pure-error
estimate $0.5$ of $\sigma^2$. The fitted curve levels off at $\hat\beta_0 = 12.4$\,g, the estimated
yield at very high concentrations.
\end{solution}

\begin{exa}\label{exa:bacteria}
The count $y$ of a bacterial culture (thousands of cells per ml) was recorded hourly:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l|cccccccc}
Hour $t$ & 0 & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6 & 7 \\
\hline
Count $y$ & 10 & 17 & 25 & 32 & 48 & 70 & 117 & 164 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Fit a suitable model and predict the count at $t = 8$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\emph{A straight line first.} Least squares gives $\hat y = -11.67 + 20.58t$, with $R^2 = 0.861$. The
residuals,
\[
21.7,\; 8.1,\; -4.5,\; -18.1,\; -22.7,\; -21.3,\; 5.2,\; 31.6 ,
\]
are positive at both ends and negative in the middle: the curvature pattern of
Figure~\ref{fig:resid-patterns}. The residuals also grow in size with the fitted value. A
culture grows by a roughly constant percentage each hour, which suggests the exponential model
with multiplicative error, $y = \beta_0 e^{\beta_1 t}\varepsilon$.

\emph{The log model.} Let $y' = \ln y$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l|cccccccc}
$t$ & 0 & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6 & 7 \\
\hline
$\ln y$ & 2.3026 & 2.8332 & 3.2189 & 3.4657 & 3.8712 & 4.2485 & 4.7622 & 5.0999 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
$\sum \ln y_i = 29.8021$, so $\overline{y'} = 3.72527$; $\bar t = 3.5$, $S_{tt} = 42$, and
$S_{ty'} = \sum(t_i - 3.5)\ln y_i = 16.3600$. Hence
\[
\hat\beta_1 = \frac{16.3600}{42} = 0.38952, \qquad
\hat\beta_0' = 3.72527 - 0.38952 \times 3.5 = 2.36194 ,
\]
\[
\widehat{\ln y} = 2.3619 + 0.3895t .
\]
The residuals on the log scale are
$-0.059$, $0.082$, $0.078$, $-0.065$, $-0.049$, $-0.061$, $0.063$, $0.011$: small, of constant size and
without a pattern. $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 0.03069$, $MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 0.005115$ and $R^2 = 0.995$.

\emph{Interpretation.} On the original scale the fitted model is
\[
\hat y = e^{2.3619}\,e^{0.3895t} = 10.61 \times 1.476^t .
\]
The (median) count is multiplied by $e^{0.3895} = 1.476$ each hour: a growth rate of about
$48\%$ per hour, and a doubling time of $\ln 2/0.3895 = 1.78$ hours.

\emph{Prediction at $t = 8$.} On the log scale, $\widehat{\ln y}_0 = 2.3619 + 0.3895 \times 8 = 5.4781$.
With $h_0 = 1/8 + (8 - 3.5)^2/42 = 0.6071$ and $t_{0.025,6} = 2.447$, the $95\%$ prediction
interval of Theorem~\ref{thm:prediction} is
\begin{align*}
5.4781 \pm 2.447\sqrt{0.005115 \times 1.6071} &= 5.4781 \pm 0.2219 \\
&= (5.2563,\ 5.7000) .
\end{align*}
Exponentiating the end points, a new count at $t = 8$ will lie between $e^{5.2563} = 192$ and
$e^{5.7000} = 299$ thousand per ml, with probability $0.95$. The point prediction
$e^{5.4781} = 239$ is the predicted median; the predicted mean,
$e^{5.4781 + 0.005115/2} = 240$, is barely different, because the error variance is so small.
The interval is not symmetric about $239$: on the original scale, the uncertainty is larger
upwards, as it should be for a quantity that grows by percentages.
\end{solution}

\subsection{The Box--Cox method}
Rather than guess a transformation, we can let the data choose one from the family of
\emph{power transformations} $y^\lambda$. Box and Cox (1964) made this precise. Some care is
needed, because the residual sums of squares from fits to $y^\lambda$ for different $\lambda$ are
on different scales and cannot be compared directly. The remedy is to rescale.

\begin{defn}
For $y_i > 0$, let $\dot y = \bigl(\prod_{i=1}^n y_i\bigr)^{1/n}$ be the geometric mean of the
responses. The \textbf{Box--Cox transformation} is
\[
y^{(\lambda)} = \begin{cases}
\dfrac{y^\lambda - 1}{\lambda\,\dot y^{\,\lambda - 1}} , & \lambda \neq 0, \\[3mm]
\dot y\ln y , & \lambda = 0 .
\end{cases}
\]
\end{defn}

Subtracting $1$ and dividing by $\lambda$ does not change the fit, apart from scaling; it makes
$y^{(\lambda)}$ vary continuously with $\lambda$, with the $\lambda = 0$ case as the limit
(Problem~\ref{prob:boxcox-limit}). Dividing by $\dot y^{\lambda - 1}$ puts every $\lambda$ on the
scale of $y$ itself, so that the residual sums of squares can be compared.

\begin{thm}\label{thm:boxcox}
Suppose that for some $\lambda$, $y_i^{(\lambda)} = \mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta + \varepsilon_i$ with
$\varepsilon_i$ independent $N(0, \sigma^2)$. Let $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(\lambda)$ be the residual sum of
squares from the least-squares fit of $y^{(\lambda)}$ on $\mathbf{X}$. Then, up to an additive
constant, the log-likelihood of the observed $y_i$, maximised over $\boldsymbol\beta$ and $\sigma^2$
for fixed $\lambda$, is
\[
L(\lambda) = -\frac{n}{2}\ln SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(\lambda) .
\]
So the maximum-likelihood estimate $\hat\lambda$ is the value that minimises $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(\lambda)$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
The density of $w_i = y_i^{(\lambda)}$ is normal; the density of $y_i$ is obtained by the change
of variables formula, multiplying by the Jacobian $|dw_i/dy_i|$. For $\lambda \neq 0$,
\[
\frac{dw_i}{dy_i} = \frac{\lambda y_i^{\lambda - 1}}{\lambda\,\dot y^{\,\lambda - 1}} = \left(\frac{y_i}{\dot y}\right)^{\lambda - 1} ,
\]
and for $\lambda = 0$ it is $\dot y/y_i$, the same formula. The product over $i$ is
\[
\prod_{i=1}^n\left(\frac{y_i}{\dot y}\right)^{\lambda - 1} = \left(\frac{\prod y_i}{\dot y^{\,n}}\right)^{\lambda - 1} = 1^{\lambda - 1} = 1 ,
\]
by the definition of $\dot y$. So the likelihood of the $y_i$ equals the normal likelihood of
the $w_i$:
\[
\ln\ell = -\frac{n}{2}\ln(2\pi\sigma^2) - \frac{1}{2\sigma^2}\sum_{i=1}^n\bigl(w_i - \mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta\bigr)^2 .
\]
For fixed $\sigma^2$ this is maximised over $\boldsymbol\beta$ by least squares, leaving
$-\frac{n}{2}\ln(2\pi\sigma^2) - SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(\lambda)/(2\sigma^2)$. Exactly as in
Theorem~\ref{thm:mle}, this is maximised at $\hat\sigma^2 = SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(\lambda)/n$, where its value is
\[
-\frac{n}{2}\ln SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(\lambda) + \frac{n}{2}\ln n - \frac{n}{2}\ln(2\pi) - \frac{n}{2} .
\]
The last three terms do not depend on $\lambda$.
\end{proof}

Without the factor $\dot y^{\lambda - 1}$ the Jacobian would be $\prod y_i^{\lambda - 1}$, which
depends on $\lambda$, and minimising the raw residual sum of squares would give the wrong answer.
The normalisation absorbs the Jacobian.

\subhead{A confidence interval for $\lambda$}
By the large-sample theory of likelihood ratios, $2\bigl[L(\hat\lambda) - L(\lambda)\bigr]$ is
approximately $\chi^2_1$ when $\lambda$ is the true value. So an approximate $100(1 - \alpha)\%$
confidence interval is the set of $\lambda$ with $L(\lambda) \ge L(\hat\lambda) - \frac12\chi^2_{\alpha,1}$, that is,
with
\[
SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(\lambda) \le SS^* = SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(\hat\lambda)\, e^{\chi^2_{\alpha,1}/n} .
\]
In practice $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(\lambda)$ is computed for a grid of values, say from $-2$ to $2$, and
plotted. One then chooses a \emph{simple} $\lambda$ inside the interval, such as $0$, $\frac12$ or $1$,
in preference to the exact minimiser: a transformation such as $y^{0.43}$ is hard to interpret,
and the data rarely distinguish it from $\sqrt{y}$. If $1$ is inside the interval, there is no
evidence that any transformation is needed.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:bacteria-boxcox}
Apply the Box--Cox method to the bacteria data of Example~\ref{exa:bacteria}.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
The geometric mean is $\dot y = \exp(\overline{\ln y}) = e^{3.72527} = 41.482$. For each $\lambda$,
form $y^{(\lambda)}$ and regress it on $t$. For instance, at $\lambda = 0.5$,
$y^{(0.5)} = (\sqrt{y} - 1)/(0.5 \times 41.482^{-0.5}) = 12.881(\sqrt{y} - 1)$, and at $\lambda = 0$,
$y^{(0)} = 41.482\ln y$, whose residual sum of squares is $41.482^2 \times 0.03069 = 52.81$. At
$\lambda = 1$, $y^{(1)} = y - 1$, which has the same residuals as $y$. The results:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|ccccccc}
$\lambda$ & $-1$ & $-0.5$ & $-0.25$ & $0$ & $0.25$ & $0.5$ & $1$ \\
\hline
$SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(\lambda)$ & 3223.4 & 609.0 & 172.1 & 52.8 & 194.5 & 620.4 & 2871.6 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
A finer search puts the minimum at $\hat\lambda = -0.01$, with $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(\hat\lambda) = 52.44$.
With $n = 8$ and $\chi^2_{0.05,1} = 3.841$,
\[
SS^* = 52.44\, e^{3.841/8} = 52.44 \times 1.616 = 84.76 ,
\]
and $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(\lambda) \le 84.76$ for $-0.14 \le \lambda \le 0.11$. The interval contains $0$ and
excludes both $\frac12$ and $1$: the log transformation is the right choice, and neither the
square root nor the untransformed response is acceptable. This confirms the choice made in
Example~\ref{exa:bacteria} on physical grounds.
\end{solution}

\begin{remark}[Transforming the regressors]
Regressors can be transformed as well (Example~\ref{exa:catalyst-recip}). A
transformation of $x$ changes the shape of the fitted relationship but not the error structure, so it is the remedy
for curvature when the variance is already constant, and a transformation of $y$ is the remedy
when it is not. The partial residual plots of Section~4 are the main guide to the form in which
each regressor should enter.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Weighted least squares}
Suppose the errors are uncorrelated but have unequal variances,
\[
\operatorname{Var}(\varepsilon_i) = \frac{\sigma^2}{w_i} , \qquad i = 1, \dots, n ,
\]
with known positive \textbf{weights} $w_i$. Observations with large $w_i$ are precise; those
with small $w_i$ are noisy. Ordinary least squares treats them all alike. It seems right to
give the precise observations more say, and to minimise the \emph{weighted} sum of squares
\[
S_w(\boldsymbol\beta) = \sum_{i=1}^n w_i\bigl(y_i - \mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta\bigr)^2
= (\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta)^\top\mathbf{W}(\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta) ,
\]
where $\mathbf{W} = \operatorname{diag}(w_1, \dots, w_n)$. In matrix form,
$\operatorname{Var}(\boldsymbol\varepsilon) = \sigma^2\mathbf{W}^{-1}$.

\begin{thm}[Weighted least squares]\label{thm:wls}
Under A1, A3 and $\operatorname{Var}(\boldsymbol\varepsilon) = \sigma^2\mathbf{W}^{-1}$, with $\mathbf{X}$ of full
column rank:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $S_w$ is minimised by
\[
\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_W = (\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{W}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{W}\mathbf{y} ;
\]
\item $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_W$ is unbiased, with $\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_W) = \sigma^2(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{W}\mathbf{X})^{-1}$;
\item $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_W$ has the smallest variance, for every coefficient, among all
linear unbiased estimators of $\boldsymbol\beta$;
\item $\hat\sigma^2_W = S_w(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_W)/(n - p)$ is unbiased for $\sigma^2$, and under normality all
the tests and intervals of Section~3 hold with $\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{W}\mathbf{X}$ in place of
$\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X}$ and $\hat\sigma^2_W$ in place of $MS_{\mathrm{Res}}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Let $\mathbf{W}^{1/2} = \operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{w_1}, \dots, \sqrt{w_n})$, and transform the model by
multiplying through by it:
\[
\mathbf{z} = \mathbf{W}^{1/2}\mathbf{y}, \qquad \mathbf{B} = \mathbf{W}^{1/2}\mathbf{X}, \qquad
\mathbf{z} = \mathbf{B}\boldsymbol\beta + \boldsymbol\delta, \quad \boldsymbol\delta = \mathbf{W}^{1/2}\boldsymbol\varepsilon .
\]
That is, $z_i = \sqrt{w_i}\,y_i$, and each row of $\mathbf{X}$ is multiplied by $\sqrt{w_i}$. By
Lemma~\ref{lem:linear-rv}, $E(\boldsymbol\delta) = \mathbf{0}$ and
\[
\operatorname{Var}(\boldsymbol\delta) = \mathbf{W}^{1/2}(\sigma^2\mathbf{W}^{-1})\mathbf{W}^{1/2} = \sigma^2\mathbf{I} .
\]
So the transformed model satisfies A1--A3, and $\mathbf{B}$ has full column rank because
$\mathbf{W}^{1/2}$ is invertible. Everything proved for ordinary least squares applies to it.

(i) $S_w(\boldsymbol\beta) = \|\mathbf{W}^{1/2}(\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\boldsymbol\beta)\|^2 = \|\mathbf{z} - \mathbf{B}\boldsymbol\beta\|^2$, the
ordinary sum of squares of the transformed model, minimised by Theorem~\ref{thm:ls-matrix} at
$(\mathbf{B}^\top\mathbf{B})^{-1}\mathbf{B}^\top\mathbf{z} = (\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{W}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{W}\mathbf{y}$.

(ii) By Theorem~\ref{thm:beta-props} applied to the transformed model, with
$(\mathbf{B}^\top\mathbf{B})^{-1} = (\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{W}\mathbf{X})^{-1}$.

(iii) A linear estimator $\mathbf{Ay}$ of $\boldsymbol\beta$ is $\mathbf{AW}^{-1/2}\mathbf{z}$, a linear function of
$\mathbf{z}$, and conversely; unbiasedness means the same in both. So the linear unbiased
estimators are the same whether written in terms of $\mathbf{y}$ or of $\mathbf{z}$, and
Theorem~\ref{thm:gauss-markov-general} for the transformed model says that its least-squares
estimator, $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_W$, is best among them.

(iv) $S_w(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_W)$ is the residual sum of squares of the transformed model, so
Theorems~\ref{thm:ssres-matrix} and~\ref{thm:mlr-dist} apply to it directly, and the tests and
intervals are those of the transformed model.
\end{proof}

The proof is also the practical recipe: multiply each $y_i$ and each row of $\mathbf{X}$,
including the column of ones, by $\sqrt{w_i}$, and fit by ordinary least squares
\emph{without} an intercept (the old intercept column has become $\sqrt{w_i}$, which plays its
part). Residual analysis should use the \textbf{weighted residuals}
$\sqrt{w_i}\,(y_i - \hat y_i)$, the residuals of the transformed model, which have constant
variance if the weights are right.

\begin{remark}[Generalised least squares]
The same argument works when the errors are correlated, $\operatorname{Var}(\boldsymbol\varepsilon) = \sigma^2\mathbf{V}$
with $\mathbf{V}$ any known positive definite matrix. By the spectral theorem
$\mathbf{V} = \mathbf{P}\boldsymbol\Lambda\mathbf{P}^\top$, and $\mathbf{K} = \mathbf{P}\boldsymbol\Lambda^{1/2}\mathbf{P}^\top$
is a symmetric matrix with $\mathbf{K}^2 = \mathbf{V}$. Multiplying through by $\mathbf{K}^{-1}$ gives a
model with covariance $\sigma^2\mathbf{I}$, and the best linear unbiased estimator is
\[
\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_{\mathrm{GLS}} = (\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{V}^{-1}\mathbf{y} .
\]
Weighted least squares is the case $\mathbf{V} = \mathbf{W}^{-1}$. The case of errors correlated in
time is taken up in the section on autocorrelated errors.
\end{remark}

In simple linear regression the weighted estimates have the familiar form, with every mean and
sum weighted.

\begin{coro}\label{coro:wls-slr}
For $y = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x + \varepsilon$ with weights $w_i$, let
$\bar x_w = \sum w_i x_i/\sum w_i$ and $\bar y_w = \sum w_i y_i/\sum w_i$. Then
\[
\hat\beta_1 = \frac{\sum w_i(x_i - \bar x_w)(y_i - \bar y_w)}{\sum w_i(x_i - \bar x_w)^2} ,
\qquad
\hat\beta_0 = \bar y_w - \hat\beta_1\bar x_w ,
\]
and $\operatorname{Var}(\hat\beta_1) = \sigma^2\big/\sum w_i(x_i - \bar x_w)^2$.
\end{coro}

\begin{proof}
Setting the partial derivatives of $S_w = \sum w_i(y_i - \beta_0 - \beta_1 x_i)^2$ to zero gives
$\sum w_i(y_i - \beta_0 - \beta_1 x_i) = 0$, which is $\beta_0 = \bar y_w - \beta_1\bar x_w$, and
$\sum w_i x_i(y_i - \beta_0 - \beta_1 x_i) = 0$. Substituting the first in the second,
$\sum w_i x_i\bigl[(y_i - \bar y_w) - \beta_1(x_i - \bar x_w)\bigr] = 0$. Since
$\sum w_i(y_i - \bar y_w) = \sum w_i(x_i - \bar x_w) = 0$, $x_i$ may be replaced by $x_i - \bar x_w$,
which gives $\hat\beta_1$. The variance is the $(2,2)$ entry of $\sigma^2(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{W}\mathbf{X})^{-1}$,
found exactly as in Example~\ref{exa:slr-matrix} with every sum weighted.
\end{proof}

\subhead{What happens if the weights are ignored}
Ordinary least squares remains unbiased when the variances are unequal, since unbiasedness used
only A1. But it is no longer the best linear unbiased estimator, and, more seriously, its usual
standard errors are wrong. By Lemma~\ref{lem:linear-rv}, with
$\hat{\boldsymbol\beta} = \mathbf{C}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{y}$,
\[
\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}) = \sigma^2\,\mathbf{C}\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{W}^{-1}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{C} ,
\]
not $\sigma^2\mathbf{C}$, and $MS_{\mathrm{Res}}$ no longer estimates a common $\sigma^2$. The $t$-tests
and intervals computed as if the variance were constant can then be badly misleading.

\subhead{Choosing the weights}
Weighted least squares needs the weights, at least up to a common constant. They come from
three sources.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \emph{Known from the way the data arose.} If $y_i$ is the mean of $n_i$ independent
observations, each with variance $\sigma^2$, then $\operatorname{Var}(y_i) = \sigma^2/n_i$ and
$w_i = n_i$.
\item \emph{A known form of dependence on a regressor.} If the standard deviation is
proportional to $x_i$, then $w_i = 1/x_i^2$ (Problem~\ref{prob:wls-x}). The form may be
suggested by the funnel in a residual plot against $x$.
\item \emph{Estimated.} Fit by ordinary least squares, regress the absolute residuals (or the
squared residuals) on $x$ or on $\hat y$ to estimate how the spread changes, take $w_i$ as the
reciprocal of the fitted variance, and refit. With replicates at each $x$, the sample variance
at each $x$ can be used. Estimated weights are themselves uncertain, so small samples call for
caution.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{thm}\label{thm:means-wls}
Suppose that at each of $m$ settings $\mathbf{x}_1, \dots, \mathbf{x}_m$ there are $n_i$ observations
$y_{i1}, \dots, y_{in_i}$, with means $\bar y_i$. The ordinary least-squares fit to all
$n = \sum n_i$ observations is the same as the weighted least-squares fit to the $m$ means
with weights $w_i = n_i$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
As in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:lof-identity}, for any $\boldsymbol\beta$,
\[
\sum_{i=1}^m\sum_{j=1}^{n_i}\bigl(y_{ij} - \mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta\bigr)^2
= \sum_{i=1}^m\sum_{j=1}^{n_i}(y_{ij} - \bar y_i)^2 + \sum_{i=1}^m n_i\bigl(\bar y_i - \mathbf{x}_i^\top\boldsymbol\beta\bigr)^2 ,
\]
the cross term vanishing because deviations from $\bar y_i$ add to zero. The first sum does not
involve $\boldsymbol\beta$, so the left side and the weighted sum of squares on the right are
minimised by the same $\boldsymbol\beta$.
\end{proof}

So when only the group means survive, the individual-level fit can still be recovered exactly,
provided the means are weighted by the group sizes.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:wls-means}
A process was run at five temperature settings $x$, with $1$, $4$, $6$, $4$ and $1$ runs, and
only the mean response $\bar y_i$ at each setting was kept:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l|ccccc}
Setting $x$ & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 \\
\hline
Runs $n_i$ & 1 & 4 & 6 & 4 & 1 \\
Mean $\bar y_i$ & 2 & 7 & 8 & 9 & 14 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Fit the line by weighted least squares, and compare with ordinary least squares on the five means.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
\emph{Weighted.} $w_i = n_i$, so $\sum w_i = 16$,
\[
\bar x_w = \frac{1 + 8 + 18 + 16 + 5}{16} = 3, \qquad
\bar y_w = \frac{2 + 28 + 48 + 36 + 14}{16} = 8 ,
\]
\begin{align*}
\textstyle\sum w_i(x_i - 3)^2 &= 1(4) + 4(1) + 0 + 4(1) + 1(4) = 16, \\
\textstyle\sum w_i(x_i - 3)\bar y_i &= 1(-2)(2) + 4(-1)(7) + 0 + 4(1)(9) + 1(2)(14) = 32 .
\end{align*}
So $\hat\beta_1 = 32/16 = 2$, $\hat\beta_0 = 8 - 2 \times 3 = 2$, and
\[
\hat y_W = 2 + 2x .
\]
The fitted values are $4$, $6$, $8$, $10$, $12$ and the weighted residual sum of squares is
\[
S_w = 1(-2)^2 + 4(1)^2 + 6(0)^2 + 4(-1)^2 + 1(2)^2 = 16 ,
\]
so $\hat\sigma^2_W = 16/3 = 5.33$, and $\operatorname{se}(\hat\beta_1) = \sqrt{5.33/16} = 0.577$. By
Theorem~\ref{thm:means-wls}, this is the line that would have been fitted to all sixteen runs.

\emph{Unweighted.} $\bar x = 3$, $\bar y = 8$, $S_{xx} = 10$ and
$S_{xy} = -2(2) - 1(7) + 0 + 1(9) + 2(14) = 26$, so $\hat\beta_1 = 2.6$, $\hat\beta_0 = 8 - 7.8 = 0.2$:
\[
\hat y = 0.2 + 2.6x .
\]
\emph{Comparison.} The two end settings, each a single run and so the least reliable, lie
furthest from the trend of the well-replicated middle three. Ordinary least squares, giving them
full weight, tilts the line towards them. Weighted least squares gives them a sixth of the
weight of the middle setting, and follows the precise means. How much precision is at stake? For
the slope, Corollary~\ref{coro:wls-slr} gives $\operatorname{Var}(\hat\beta_{1W}) = \sigma^2/16 = 0.0625\sigma^2$.
The ordinary estimator is $\hat\beta_1 = \sum(x_i - 3)\bar y_i/10$, with
$\operatorname{Var}(\bar y_i) = \sigma^2/n_i$, so
\[
\operatorname{Var}(\hat\beta_1) = \frac{\sigma^2}{100}\left(\frac{4}{1} + \frac{1}{4} + 0 + \frac{1}{4} + \frac{4}{1}\right) = 0.085\sigma^2 .
\]
Ignoring the weights costs a quarter of the precision: the unweighted estimate behaves as if it
had been based on $0.0625/0.085 = 74\%$ of the data.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Practice problems}
\begin{problem}\label{prob:delta-poisson}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Use the delta method to show that if $y$ is Poisson with mean $\mu$, then $\sqrt{y}$ has
variance approximately $\frac14$, whatever $\mu$.
\item Find a transformation that stabilises the variance when $\sigma^2(\mu) = \mu + \mu^2$
(counts with extra variation).
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) $g(y) = \sqrt y$ has $g'(\mu) = 1/(2\sqrt\mu)$, so by Lemma~\ref{lem:delta}
$\operatorname{Var}(\sqrt y) \approx \mu/(4\mu) = \frac14$.

(b) We need $g'(\mu) = 1/\sqrt{\mu + \mu^2}$. Substituting $\mu = u^2$, $d\mu = 2u\,du$,
\[
g(\mu) = \int\frac{d\mu}{\sqrt{\mu(1 + \mu)}} = \int\frac{2u\,du}{u\sqrt{1 + u^2}} = 2\operatorname{arsinh}u = 2\operatorname{arsinh}\sqrt\mu .
\]
So $g(y) = \operatorname{arsinh}\sqrt{y} = \ln\bigl(\sqrt y + \sqrt{1 + y}\bigr)$. For small $\mu$ it
behaves like $\sqrt y$, and for large $\mu$ like $\frac12\ln y + \ln 2$: between the square root and
the log, as the variance function is between Poisson and constant-percentage.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:boxcox-limit}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Show that $(y^\lambda - 1)/\lambda \to \ln y$ as $\lambda \to 0$, so that the Box--Cox
transformation is continuous in $\lambda$.
\item Show that $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(1)$ equals the residual sum of squares of the
untransformed fit,
and that for any $\lambda \neq 0$, $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(\lambda)$ is $1/(\lambda\dot y^{\lambda - 1})^2$ times the
residual sum of squares from regressing $y^\lambda$ itself.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) Write $y^\lambda = e^{\lambda\ln y}$. The limit is $0/0$, and by l'H\^opital's rule (differentiating
in $\lambda$) it equals $\lim_{\lambda \to 0}\ln y\,e^{\lambda\ln y} = \ln y$. Since also $\dot y^{\lambda - 1} \to \dot y^{-1}$,
$y^{(\lambda)} \to \ln y/\dot y^{-1} = \dot y\ln y = y^{(0)}$.

(b) $y^{(1)} = y - 1$. Subtracting a constant from every response changes only the intercept,
so the residuals, and $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}$, are unchanged. In general
$y^{(\lambda)} = a y^\lambda + b$ with $a = 1/(\lambda\dot y^{\lambda - 1})$ and $b = -a$. Adding $b$ leaves the
residuals alone, and multiplying the response by $a$ multiplies $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$, the fitted
values and the residuals by $a$ (the least-squares estimator is linear in $\mathbf{y}$), so
$SS_{\mathrm{Res}}$ is multiplied by $a^2$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:power}
The time $y$ (minutes) to dry a coating of thickness $x$ (mm) was measured:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l|ccccc}
$x$ & 1 & 2 & 4 & 8 & 16 \\
\hline
$y$ & 3 & 4 & 6 & 9 & 12 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Fit the power model $y = \beta_0 x^{\beta_1}\varepsilon$ and interpret $\beta_1$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Let $u = \ln x$ and $v = \ln y$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l|ccccc}
$u = \ln x$ & 0 & 0.6931 & 1.3863 & 2.0794 & 2.7726 \\
$v = \ln y$ & 1.0986 & 1.3863 & 1.7918 & 2.1972 & 2.4849 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The $u_i$ are $0$, $1$, $2$, $3$, $4$ times $\ln 2 = 0.6931$, so $\bar u = 2\ln 2 = 1.3863$ and
$S_{uu} = 10(\ln 2)^2 = 4.8045$. Then $\bar v = 8.9588/5 = 1.79176$ and
\begin{align*}
S_{uv} &= \ln 2\,\bigl[-2(1.0986) - 1(1.3863) + 0 + 1(2.1972) + 2(2.4849)\bigr] \\
&= 0.6931 \times 3.5835 = 2.4839 ,
\end{align*}
so $\hat\beta_1 = 2.4839/4.8045 = 0.517$, and $\widehat{\ln\beta_0} = 1.79176 - 0.517 \times 1.3863 = 1.0751$,
giving $\hat\beta_0 = e^{1.0751} = 2.93$:
\[
\hat y = 2.93\,x^{0.517} .
\]
$\beta_1$ is an \emph{elasticity}: a $1\%$ increase in thickness goes with an increase of about
$\beta_1\% = 0.52\%$ in drying time; doubling the thickness multiplies the drying time by
$2^{0.517} = 1.43$. The estimate is close to $\frac12$, which would mean drying time proportional
to the square root of thickness, a hypothesis that could be tested with the $t$ statistic for
$\beta_1 = 0.5$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:wls-x}
Suppose $y_i = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x_i + \varepsilon_i$ with $x_i > 0$ and $\operatorname{Var}(\varepsilon_i) = \sigma^2x_i^2$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Show that weighted least squares with $w_i = 1/x_i^2$ is the same as ordinary least squares
for the model $y_i/x_i = \beta_1 + \beta_0(1/x_i) + \delta_i$, in which the roles of intercept and slope
are exchanged, and that the $\delta_i$ have constant variance.
\item Fit both ways to the data $x = 1, 2, 3, 4$, $y = 2, 4, 6, 11$, and compare with ordinary least
squares on the original data.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) Dividing the model by $x_i$, which is multiplying by $\sqrt{w_i} = 1/x_i$, gives
$y_i/x_i = \beta_0/x_i + \beta_1 + \varepsilon_i/x_i$, and $\operatorname{Var}(\varepsilon_i/x_i) = \sigma^2$. This is the
transformed model in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:wls}: its regressor columns are $1/x_i$ (from the
intercept) and $x_i/x_i = 1$ (from the slope). So the old slope $\beta_1$ is the new intercept, and the
old intercept $\beta_0$ the new slope.

(b) Let $u = 1/x$ and $v = y/x$: $u = 1, \frac12, \frac13, \frac14$ and $v = 2, 2, 2, 2.75$. Then
$\bar u = 25/48 = 0.52083$, $\bar v = 2.1875$,
\begin{align*}
S_{uu} &= \textstyle\sum u_i^2 - 4\bar u^2 = 1.42361 - 1.08507 = 0.33854, \\
S_{uv} &= \textstyle\sum (u_i - \bar u)v_i \\
&= 0.95833 - 0.04167 - 0.37500 - 0.74479 = -0.20313 ,
\end{align*}
so the new slope is $-0.20313/0.33854 = -0.600$ and the new intercept
$2.1875 + 0.600 \times 0.52083 = 2.500$. Hence $\hat\beta_0 = -0.6$, $\hat\beta_1 = 2.5$:
\[
\hat y_W = -0.6 + 2.5x .
\]
Corollary~\ref{coro:wls-slr} gives the same: with $w = 1, \frac14, \frac19, \frac1{16}$ the weighted means
are $\bar x_w = 2.0833/1.4236 = 1.4634$ and $\bar y_w = 4.3542/1.4236 = 3.0585$, and the
slope is $2.5$. Ordinary least squares gives
$\bar x = 2.5$, $\bar y = 5.75$, $S_{xx} = 5$ and $S_{xy} = 14.5$, so
$\hat y = -1.5 + 2.9x$. The last
point, $(4, 11)$, has the largest variance under the model; ordinary least squares lets it raise the
slope, while the weighted fit discounts it.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:wls-resid}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Show that the weighted residuals $e_i = y_i - \hat y_i$ of a weighted fit with an intercept
satisfy $\sum w_i e_i = 0$ and $\sum w_i x_{ij}e_i = 0$, but in general $\sum e_i \neq 0$.
\item Check this on Example~\ref{exa:wls-means}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) The normal equations of Theorem~\ref{thm:wls} are $\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{W}(\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_W) = \mathbf{0}$,
whose rows are $\sum w_i x_{ij}e_i = 0$, the first ($x_{i0} = 1$) being $\sum w_i e_i = 0$. These are
weighted sums; the unweighted sum $\sum e_i$ is not constrained.

(b) The residuals are $-2$, $1$, $0$, $-1$, $2$ with weights $1$, $4$, $6$, $4$, $1$:
$\sum w_ie_i = -2 + 4 + 0 - 4 + 2 = 0$ and $\sum w_ix_ie_i = -2 + 8 + 0 - 16 + 10 = 0$. Here
$\sum e_i = 0$ too, by the symmetry of the design, but with the data of Problem~\ref{prob:wls-x} the
residuals from $-0.6 + 2.5x$ are $0.1$, $-0.4$, $-0.9$, $1.6$, which add to $0.4$.
\end{solution}

\section{Selecting the Best Regression Model}
So far the regressors in a model have been given. In practice there is usually a list of
candidates, some of which matter and some of which do not, and part of the analysis is to decide
which to keep. This is the \textbf{variable selection} problem. Two forces pull against each
other. Leaving out a regressor that matters biases the estimates of the others and the
predictions. Putting in regressors that do not matter costs degrees of freedom and inflates the
variances. A good model is a compromise: as simple as it can be while still describing the data
adequately.

This section first makes the two costs precise, then describes the criteria used to compare
candidate models, and finally the two ways of searching among them: examining every possible
subset, and stepwise methods that add or remove one regressor at a time.

Throughout, the \emph{full model} contains all $K$ candidate regressors, and a \emph{subset model}
contains $p - 1$ of them with an intercept, so $p$ parameters. Model adequacy checking
(Section~4) should be done on the full model first; selection among regressors assumes that the
form of the model is otherwise right.

\subsection{The cost of leaving regressors out, and of putting them in}
Partition the full model as
\[
\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{X}_1\boldsymbol\beta_1 + \mathbf{X}_2\boldsymbol\beta_2 + \boldsymbol\varepsilon ,
\]
where $\mathbf{X}_1$ ($n \times p$, including the column of ones) holds the regressors to be kept and
$\mathbf{X}_2$ ($n \times r$) the regressors to be deleted. Assume the full model is correct and
satisfies A1--A3. Let $\mathbf{H}_1$ be the hat matrix of $\mathbf{X}_1$, and write
\begin{itemize}
\item $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1$, $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2$ for the least-squares estimates in the full model;
\item $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1^* = (\mathbf{X}_1^\top\mathbf{X}_1)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_1^\top\mathbf{y}$ for the estimate of
$\boldsymbol\beta_1$ from the subset model, which ignores $\mathbf{X}_2$;
\item $\mathbf{A} = (\mathbf{X}_1^\top\mathbf{X}_1)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_1^\top\mathbf{X}_2$, the $p \times r$ \textbf{alias matrix},
whose column $j$ holds the coefficients from regressing column $j$ of $\mathbf{X}_2$ on $\mathbf{X}_1$.
\end{itemize}

\begin{lem}\label{lem:partitioned}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1^* = \hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1 + \mathbf{A}\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2$;
\item $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2 = \bigl(\mathbf{X}_2^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{X}_2\bigr)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_2^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{y}$;
\item $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1^*$ and $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2$ are uncorrelated.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
The normal equations of the full model, split into the rows belonging to $\mathbf{X}_1$ and to
$\mathbf{X}_2$, are
\begin{align*}
\mathbf{X}_1^\top\mathbf{X}_1\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1 + \mathbf{X}_1^\top\mathbf{X}_2\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2 &= \mathbf{X}_1^\top\mathbf{y} , \\
\mathbf{X}_2^\top\mathbf{X}_1\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1 + \mathbf{X}_2^\top\mathbf{X}_2\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2 &= \mathbf{X}_2^\top\mathbf{y} .
\end{align*}
(i) Multiply the first by $(\mathbf{X}_1^\top\mathbf{X}_1)^{-1}$:
$\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1 + \mathbf{A}\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2 = \hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1^*$.

(ii) Substitute $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1 = \hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1^* - \mathbf{A}\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2$ in the second. Since
$\mathbf{X}_1\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1^* = \mathbf{H}_1\mathbf{y}$ and $\mathbf{X}_1\mathbf{A} = \mathbf{H}_1\mathbf{X}_2$,
\[
\mathbf{X}_2^\top\mathbf{H}_1\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}_2^\top\mathbf{H}_1\mathbf{X}_2\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2 + \mathbf{X}_2^\top\mathbf{X}_2\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2 = \mathbf{X}_2^\top\mathbf{y} ,
\]
that is, $\mathbf{X}_2^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{X}_2\,\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2 = \mathbf{X}_2^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{y}$. The matrix on
the left is invertible because $\mathbf{X}$ has full column rank (the argument is that of
Theorem~\ref{thm:ls-matrix}, applied to $(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{X}_2$).

(iii) By Lemma~\ref{lem:linear-rv}, the covariance of
$(\mathbf{X}_1^\top\mathbf{X}_1)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_1^\top\mathbf{y}$ with $\mathbf{M}\mathbf{X}_2^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{y}$, where
$\mathbf{M}$ is the inverse in (ii), is
$\sigma^2(\mathbf{X}_1^\top\mathbf{X}_1)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_1^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{X}_2\mathbf{M} = \mathbf{0}$, because
$\mathbf{X}_1^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1) = \bigl((\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{X}_1\bigr)^\top = \mathbf{0}$.
\end{proof}

Part (ii) is the matrix form of the added-variable theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:added-variable}), which
was the case $r = 1$.

\begin{thm}[Underfitting and overfitting]\label{thm:under-over}
Suppose the full model is correct. Then
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $E(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1^*) = \boldsymbol\beta_1 + \mathbf{A}\boldsymbol\beta_2$, so the subset estimate is biased unless
$\boldsymbol\beta_2 = \mathbf{0}$ or $\mathbf{A} = \mathbf{0}$;
\item $\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1) = \operatorname{Var}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1^*) + \mathbf{A}\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2)\mathbf{A}^\top$; in
particular each $\operatorname{Var}(\hat\beta_{1j}) \ge \operatorname{Var}(\hat\beta^*_{1j})$;
\item the residual mean square of the subset model has
\[
E\bigl(MS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)\bigr) = \sigma^2 + \frac{\boldsymbol\beta_2^\top\mathbf{X}_2^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{X}_2\boldsymbol\beta_2}{n - p} \;\ge\; \sigma^2 ;
\]
\item the mean square error matrix of $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1^*$,
$E\bigl[(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1^* - \boldsymbol\beta_1)(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1^* - \boldsymbol\beta_1)^\top\bigr]$, equals
$\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1) - \mathbf{A}\bigl[\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2) - \boldsymbol\beta_2\boldsymbol\beta_2^\top\bigr]\mathbf{A}^\top$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
(i) $E(\mathbf{y}) = \mathbf{X}_1\boldsymbol\beta_1 + \mathbf{X}_2\boldsymbol\beta_2$, so
$E(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1^*) = (\mathbf{X}_1^\top\mathbf{X}_1)^{-1}\mathbf{X}_1^\top(\mathbf{X}_1\boldsymbol\beta_1 + \mathbf{X}_2\boldsymbol\beta_2) = \boldsymbol\beta_1 + \mathbf{A}\boldsymbol\beta_2$.

(ii) By Lemma~\ref{lem:partitioned}(i), $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1 = \hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1^* - \mathbf{A}\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2$, a difference of
uncorrelated vectors by part (iii) of the lemma, so the variances add. The diagonal entries of
$\mathbf{A}\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2)\mathbf{A}^\top$ are variances of linear combinations of $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2$, and so
are $\ge 0$.

(iii) Write $\boldsymbol\mu = \mathbf{X}_1\boldsymbol\beta_1 + \mathbf{X}_2\boldsymbol\beta_2$.
$SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p) = \mathbf{y}^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{y}$, and by Lemma~\ref{lem:quad-form}
\begin{align*}
E\bigl(SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)\bigr) &= \sigma^2\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1) + \boldsymbol\mu^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\boldsymbol\mu \\
&= (n - p)\sigma^2 + \boldsymbol\beta_2^\top\mathbf{X}_2^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{X}_2\boldsymbol\beta_2 ,
\end{align*}
since $(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{X}_1 = \mathbf{0}$. The quadratic form is $\|(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\mathbf{X}_2\boldsymbol\beta_2\|^2 \ge 0$.

(iv) The mean square error matrix is the variance plus the bias times its transpose:
$\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1^*) + \mathbf{A}\boldsymbol\beta_2\boldsymbol\beta_2^\top\mathbf{A}^\top$. Substitute
$\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1^*) = \operatorname{Var}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1) - \mathbf{A}\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2)\mathbf{A}^\top$ from (ii).
\end{proof}

The theorem says three things.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Underfitting biases.} Deleting regressors that matter biases the remaining
coefficients, through the alias matrix: the kept regressors take credit for the omitted ones in
proportion to their correlation with them. It also biases $MS_{\mathrm{Res}}$ upwards, so the
subset model looks noisier than the truth.
\item \emph{Overfitting inflates variance.} If $\boldsymbol\beta_2 = \mathbf{0}$, the subset model is correct
and unbiased, and part (ii) shows that carrying the useless regressors along only increases the
variances of the others.
\item \emph{The trade-off.} By (iv), deleting $\mathbf{X}_2$ reduces the mean square error of the
estimates of $\boldsymbol\beta_1$ whenever $\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_2) - \boldsymbol\beta_2\boldsymbol\beta_2^\top$ is positive semi-definite.
For a single deleted regressor this says $\beta_2^2 \le \operatorname{Var}(\hat\beta_2)$: it pays to drop a
regressor whose true coefficient is smaller than the standard error with which it could be
estimated. A biased model can be the more accurate one.
\end{itemize}

\begin{exa}\label{exa:farms}
A survey of twelve farms recorded the maize yield $y$ (bags per hectare), the fertiliser applied
$x_1$ (bags per hectare), the labour input $x_2$ (person-days per hectare, in tens) and the
distance $x_3$ from the farm to the nearest market (km):
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|cccc||c|cccc}
Farm & $x_1$ & $x_2$ & $x_3$ & $y$ & Farm & $x_1$ & $x_2$ & $x_3$ & $y$ \\
\hline
1 & 7 & 6 & 4 & 29 & 7 & 7 & 4 & 7 & 24 \\
2 & 1 & 2 & 4 & 9 & 8 & 5 & 2 & 8 & 18 \\
3 & 2 & 2 & 6 & 11 & 9 & 1 & 2 & 7 & 13 \\
4 & 2 & 1 & 5 & 13 & 10 & 1 & 2 & 3 & 8 \\
5 & 2 & 5 & 2 & 17 & 11 & 3 & 6 & 3 & 19 \\
6 & 7 & 8 & 6 & 29 & 12 & 4 & 5 & 6 & 22 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The correlations are $r_{12} = 0.65$, $r_{13} = 0.37$, $r_{23} = -0.21$, and with $y$: $0.93$,
$0.83$ and $0.20$. Compare the fits of $y$ on $x_1$ alone, on $x_1$ and $x_2$, and on all three.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
By the methods of Section~3 (with the matrices given at the end of this solution):
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l|ccc}
Model & $\hat\beta_1$ (se) & $\hat\beta_2$ (se) & $\hat\beta_3$ (se) \\
\hline
$x_1$ & 2.754 (0.355) & & \\
$x_1, x_2$ & 1.982 (0.289) & 1.303 (0.316) & \\
$x_1, x_2, x_3$ & 1.801 (0.407) & 1.480 (0.424) & 0.268 (0.410) \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\emph{Underfitting.} Leaving out labour raises the fertiliser coefficient from $1.98$ to $2.75$. The
alias matrix explains it: regressing $x_2$ on $x_1$ gives slope $38.5/65 = 0.592$, and by
Lemma~\ref{lem:partitioned}(i),
\[
\hat\beta_1^* = \hat\beta_1 + 0.592\,\hat\beta_2 = 1.982 + 0.592 \times 1.303 = 2.754 .
\]
Farms that use more fertiliser also tend to put in more labour, and with labour omitted, fertiliser
is credited with part of its effect. The residual mean square rises from $3.15$ to $8.17$.

\emph{Overfitting.} Adding the distance to market, whose coefficient is small and far from
significant ($t = 0.65$), raises the standard error of $\hat\beta_1$ from $0.289$ to $0.407$ and of
$\hat\beta_2$ from $0.316$ to $0.424$: distance is mildly correlated with the other two, and carrying
it along costs precision for nothing.

For the record, for the model in $x_1, x_2$,
\[
\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X} = \begin{bmatrix} 12 & 42 & 45 \\ 42 & 212 & 196 \\ 45 & 196 & 223 \end{bmatrix} ,
\qquad
\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{y} = \begin{bmatrix} 212 \\ 921 \\ 942 \end{bmatrix} ,
\]
with $\det(\mathbf{X}^\top\mathbf{X}) = 24528$, and the fit is $\hat y = 5.843 + 1.982x_1 + 1.303x_2$.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Criteria for comparing models}
Each candidate model is summarised by a number, and the numbers are compared. Write
$SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)$, $R^2_p$ and $MS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)$ for the quantities of a subset model with
$p$ parameters, and $\hat\sigma^2$ for the residual mean square of the full model.

\subhead{$R^2$, adjusted $R^2$ and the residual mean square}
$R^2_p = 1 - SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)/SS_T$ never decreases when a regressor is added
(Theorem~\ref{thm:r2-increase}), so it cannot choose between models of different sizes; it is
used to compare models of the \emph{same} size, and to see where adding regressors stops paying,
the point at which $R^2_p$ levels off as $p$ grows. The adjusted
\[
R^2_{\mathrm{adj},p} = 1 - \frac{SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)/(n - p)}{SS_T/(n - 1)} = 1 - \frac{MS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)}{SS_T/(n - 1)}
\]
does penalise size, and since $SS_T/(n - 1)$ is the same for every model, maximising
$R^2_{\mathrm{adj},p}$ is the same as minimising $MS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)$. As shown in the practice problems of
Section~3, adding one regressor raises $R^2_{\mathrm{adj}}$ exactly when its partial $F$ statistic exceeds
$1$, which is a weak requirement; these criteria tend to choose models that are too large.

\subhead{Mallows' $C_p$}
A model is used to estimate the mean response at the observed points. A natural measure of its
quality is the total mean squared error of the fitted values, scaled by $\sigma^2$:
\[
\Gamma_p = \frac{1}{\sigma^2}\sum_{i=1}^n E\bigl[(\hat y_i - E(y_i))^2\bigr] .
\]
It combines variance and bias, and it is small for a good model.

\begin{thm}\label{thm:cp}
For a subset model with $p$ parameters,
\[
\Gamma_p = p + \frac{SS_B}{\sigma^2} = \frac{E\bigl(SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)\bigr)}{\sigma^2} - n + 2p ,
\]
where $SS_B = \sum_i\bigl(E(\hat y_i) - E(y_i)\bigr)^2 = \boldsymbol\mu^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\boldsymbol\mu$ is the sum of
squared biases.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Each term splits into variance plus squared bias:
$E[(\hat y_i - \mu_i)^2] = \operatorname{Var}(\hat y_i) + (E\hat y_i - \mu_i)^2$. The fitted values are
$\hat{\mathbf{y}} = \mathbf{H}_1\mathbf{y}$, with covariance matrix $\sigma^2\mathbf{H}_1\mathbf{H}_1^\top = \sigma^2\mathbf{H}_1$, so
$\sum_i\operatorname{Var}(\hat y_i) = \sigma^2\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{H}_1) = p\sigma^2$. The biases form the vector
$\mathbf{H}_1\boldsymbol\mu - \boldsymbol\mu = -(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\boldsymbol\mu$, whose squared length is
$\boldsymbol\mu^\top(\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H}_1)\boldsymbol\mu = SS_B$. This gives the first form. By the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:under-over}(iii), $E(SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)) = (n - p)\sigma^2 + SS_B$, so
$SS_B/\sigma^2 = E(SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p))/\sigma^2 - n + p$; add $p$.
\end{proof}

\begin{defn}
\textbf{Mallows' $C_p$} is the estimate of $\Gamma_p$ obtained by replacing $E(SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p))$ by
$SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)$ and $\sigma^2$ by the full-model estimate $\hat\sigma^2$:
\[
C_p = \frac{SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)}{\hat\sigma^2} - n + 2p .
\]
\end{defn}

If a subset model has no bias, $SS_B = 0$ and $\Gamma_p = p$, so its $C_p$ should be close to $p$. A
model with substantial bias has $C_p$ well above $p$. The usual practice is to plot $C_p$ against
$p$ for every candidate, draw the line $C_p = p$, and look for models that are small, have $C_p$
near or below the line, and have a small $C_p$. For the full model $C_p = p$ exactly
(Problem~\ref{prob:cp-full}), so the criterion is only as good as the assumption that the full
model is unbiased and $\hat\sigma^2$ a fair estimate of $\sigma^2$.

\subhead{AIC and BIC}
Information criteria balance fit against size by penalising the log-likelihood. For the normal
linear model with $p$ parameters, the argument in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:boxcox} shows
that the maximised log-likelihood is $-\frac{n}{2}\ln\bigl(SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)/n\bigr)$ plus a
constant. The
\textbf{Akaike information criterion} and the \textbf{Bayesian information criterion} are
\[
\mathrm{AIC}_p = n\ln\frac{SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)}{n} + 2p ,
\qquad
\mathrm{BIC}_p = n\ln\frac{SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)}{n} + p\ln n ,
\]
and smaller is better. The first term rewards fit; the second penalises size. Akaike derived the
penalty $2p$ as the bias in the maximised log-likelihood as an estimate of how well the model will
predict new data; Schwarz derived $p\ln n$ from a large-sample approximation to the posterior
probability of the model. The derivations are beyond this course, but the consequence is easy to
see: for $n \ge 8$, $\ln n > 2$, and BIC penalises extra parameters more heavily than AIC, so it
tends to choose smaller models. As $n \to \infty$, BIC chooses the true model (if it is among the
candidates) with probability tending to $1$, while AIC aims at good prediction and may keep a few
superfluous terms.

\subhead{PRESS}
The prediction error sum of squares of Section~4,
$\mathrm{PRESS}_p = \sum e_i^2/(1 - h_{ii})^2$, measures how well a model predicts each observation
from the others. It is a direct estimate of predictive performance, needs no estimate of
$\sigma^2$, and is computed from a single fit of each candidate. Prefer models with small PRESS, or
equivalently large $R^2_{\mathrm{pred}}$.

\subsection{All possible regressions}
With $K$ candidate regressors there are $2^K$ subset models, counting the model with the
intercept alone. When $K$ is moderate (up to about $20$ with modern computing) all of them can be
fitted and compared on the criteria above. The usual presentation lists, for each size $p$, the
few best models of that size.

\begin{exa}\label{exa:farms-all}
Fit all eight subset models to the farm data of Example~\ref{exa:farms} and choose a model.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
Here $n = 12$, $SS_T = 574.67$, and the full model gives $\hat\sigma^2 = MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 26.891/8 = 3.361$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l|crrrr}
Regressors & $p$ & $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}$ & $R^2_p$ & $R^2_{\mathrm{adj}}$ & $MS_{\mathrm{Res}}$ \\
\hline
none & 1 & 574.67 & 0 & 0 & 52.24 \\
$x_1$ & 2 & 81.73 & 0.858 & 0.844 & 8.17 \\
$x_2$ & 2 & 176.34 & 0.693 & 0.663 & 17.63 \\
$x_3$ & 2 & 552.56 & 0.038 & $-0.058$ & 55.26 \\
$x_1, x_2$ & 3 & 28.33 & 0.951 & 0.940 & 3.15 \\
$x_1, x_3$ & 3 & 67.85 & 0.882 & 0.856 & 7.54 \\
$x_2, x_3$ & 3 & 92.71 & 0.839 & 0.803 & 10.30 \\
$x_1, x_2, x_3$ & 4 & 26.89 & 0.953 & 0.936 & 3.36 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l|rrrr}
Regressors & $C_p$ & AIC & BIC & PRESS \\
\hline
none & 160.96 & 48.43 & 48.91 & 683.90 \\
$x_1$ & 16.31 & 27.02 & 27.99 & 116.85 \\
$x_2$ & 44.46 & 36.25 & 37.22 & 240.42 \\
$x_3$ & 156.39 & 49.96 & 50.93 & 734.91 \\
$x_1, x_2$ & 2.43 & 16.31 & 17.76 & 46.28 \\
$x_1, x_3$ & 14.18 & 26.79 & 28.24 & 128.94 \\
$x_2, x_3$ & 21.58 & 30.53 & 31.99 & 162.92 \\
$x_1, x_2, x_3$ & 4.00 & 17.68 & 19.62 & 68.74 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Two of the entries in full, for the model in $x_1, x_2$ ($p = 3$):
\begin{align*}
C_3 &= \frac{28.332}{3.361} - 12 + 6 = 8.43 - 6 = 2.43 , \\
\mathrm{AIC}_3 &= 12\ln\frac{28.332}{12} + 6 = 12 \times 0.8591 + 6 = 16.31 .
\end{align*}
Every criterion chooses the model in $x_1$ and $x_2$. It has the largest $R^2_{\mathrm{adj}}$ and the
smallest $MS_{\mathrm{Res}}$, $C_p$, AIC, BIC and PRESS. Its $C_p = 2.43$ is below $p = 3$, so there is no
sign of bias, while every other model with fewer than four parameters has $C_p$ far above the line
(Figure~\ref{fig:cp}). Adding $x_3$ raises $R^2$ only from $0.951$ to $0.953$, and makes every other
criterion worse; PRESS in particular rises by half, from $46$ to $69$. The chosen model is
\[
\hat y = 5.84 + 1.98x_1 + 1.30x_2 .
\]
Each extra bag of fertiliser per hectare goes with about two more bags of maize, at a fixed labour
input; distance to market adds nothing once fertiliser and labour are known.
\end{solution}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.6cm,y=0.09cm]
\draw[-Stealth] (0.6,0) -- (4.6,0) node[right] {\small $p$};
\draw[-Stealth] (0.6,0) -- (0.6,52) node[above] {\small $C_p$};
\foreach \t in {1,2,3,4} \draw[-] (\t,1) -- (\t,-1) node[below] {\scriptsize $\t$};
\foreach \t in {10,20,30,40,50} \draw[-] (0.63,\t) -- (0.57,\t) node[left] {\scriptsize $\t$};
\draw[-,dashed,gray] (0.6,0.6) -- (4.4,4.4) node[above right,font=\scriptsize] {$C_p = p$};
\foreach \p/\c in {2/16.31,2/44.46,3/2.43,3/14.18,3/21.58,4/4.0}
  \fill[blue] (\p,\c) circle (2.4pt);
\node[right,font=\scriptsize] at (2,16.31) {$x_1$};
\node[right,font=\scriptsize] at (2,44.46) {$x_2$};
\node[above left,font=\scriptsize] at (3,2.43) {$x_1x_2$};
\node[right,font=\scriptsize] at (3,14.18) {$x_1x_3$};
\node[right,font=\scriptsize] at (3,21.58) {$x_2x_3$};
\node[above left,font=\scriptsize] at (4,4.0) {$x_1x_2x_3$};
\node[font=\scriptsize,align=center] at (3.6,46) {off the scale:\\ none ($161$), $x_3$ ($156$)};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The $C_p$ plot for the farm data. Models near or below the dashed line show no sign of
bias; the model in $x_1$ and $x_2$ is the smallest such model.}
\label{fig:cp}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Stepwise methods}
When there are too many candidates to fit every subset, or as a quicker first look, models are
built one regressor at a time. Every step uses the partial $F$ statistic of
Subsection~\ref{ssec:extra-ss} for a single regressor $x_j$ given the regressors already in the model,
\[
F_j = \frac{SS_R(\beta_j \mid \text{others in the model})}{MS_{\mathrm{Res}}(\text{model with } x_j)} ,
\]
which equals the square of the $t$ statistic for $\beta_j$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:added-variable}). Two
thresholds are chosen: $F_{\mathrm{in}}$, which a regressor must exceed to enter, and $F_{\mathrm{out}}$,
below which a regressor already in the model is removed. Common choices are
$F_{\mathrm{in}} = F_{\mathrm{out}} = 4$, roughly the $5\%$ point of $F_{1,\nu}$ for moderate $\nu$.
Figure~\ref{fig:stepwise} summarises the three methods.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85,every node/.style={font=\scriptsize,align=center}]
\foreach \px/\col/\num/\name/\start/\stepa/\stepb/\stopp in {
 0/blue/1/{Forward selection}/{start with no regressors}/{add the candidate with\\ the largest $F_j$}/{a regressor once in\\ is never removed}/{stop when the largest\\ $F_j < F_{\mathrm{in}}$},
 5.4/orange/2/{Backward elimination}/{start with all $K$ regressors}/{remove the regressor with\\ the smallest $F_j$}/{a regressor once out\\ never returns}/{stop when the smallest\\ $F_j \ge F_{\mathrm{out}}$},
 10.8/green/3/{Stepwise regression}/{start with no regressors}/{add as in forward\\ selection}/{after each addition, remove\\ any with $F_j < F_{\mathrm{out}}$}/{stop when no regressor\\ enters or leaves}} {
 \begin{scope}[shift={(\px,0)}]
  \filldraw[fill=\col!8,draw=\col!60!black,rounded corners=6pt] (-0.2,-5.2) rectangle (5.0,0.6);
  \node[circle,fill=\col!60!black,text=white,inner sep=1.5pt,font=\footnotesize\bfseries] at (0.15,0.25) {\num};
  \node[anchor=west,font=\footnotesize\bfseries,text=\col!50!black] at (0.4,0.25) {\name};
  \node[draw=\col!60!black,fill=white,rounded corners=3pt,minimum width=3.8cm] (a) at (2.4,-0.55) {\start};
  \node[draw=\col!60!black,fill=white,rounded corners=3pt,minimum width=3.8cm] (b) at (2.4,-1.75) {\stepa};
  \node[draw=\col!60!black,fill=white,rounded corners=3pt,minimum width=3.8cm] (c) at (2.4,-3.05) {\stepb};
  \node[draw=\col!60!black,fill=\col!20,rounded corners=3pt,minimum width=3.8cm] (d) at (2.4,-4.4) {\stopp};
  \draw[-Stealth,\col!60!black] (a) -- (b);
  \draw[-Stealth,\col!60!black] (b) -- (c);
  \draw[-Stealth,\col!60!black] (c) -- (d);
 \end{scope}
}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The three stepwise methods. Each uses the partial $F$ statistic $F_j$ of a single regressor
given those currently in the model.}
\label{fig:stepwise}
\end{figure}

\begin{exa}\label{exa:farms-stepwise}
Apply forward selection and backward elimination to the farm data, with
$F_{\mathrm{in}} = F_{\mathrm{out}} = 4$.
\end{exa}

\begin{solution}
All the residual sums of squares are in the table of Example~\ref{exa:farms-all}.

\emph{Forward selection.} Step~1: with no regressors, $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 574.67$. Adding $x_1$ gives
$F = (574.67 - 81.73)/(81.73/10) = 60.3$; adding $x_2$, $(574.67 - 176.34)/17.63 = 22.6$; adding $x_3$,
$(574.67 - 552.56)/55.26 = 0.40$. The largest is $60.3 > 4$, so $x_1$ enters.
Step~2: given $x_1$,
\begin{align*}
x_2:\quad F &= \frac{81.73 - 28.33}{28.33/9} = \frac{53.40}{3.148} = 16.96, \\
x_3:\quad F &= \frac{81.73 - 67.85}{67.85/9} = \frac{13.88}{7.539} = 1.84 ,
\end{align*}
so $x_2$ enters. Step~3: given $x_1$ and $x_2$, adding $x_3$ gives
$F = (28.33 - 26.89)/(26.89/8) = 1.44/3.361 = 0.43 < 4$. Stop. The model is $x_1, x_2$.
(In stepwise regression we would also check, after $x_2$ entered, whether $x_1$ should leave:
$F = (176.34 - 28.33)/3.148 = 47.0$, so it stays, and the result is the same.)

\emph{Backward elimination.} Start from the full model, $MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 3.361$. Deleting each
regressor in turn:
\begin{align*}
x_1:\quad F &= (92.71 - 26.89)/3.361 = 19.58, \\
x_2:\quad F &= (67.85 - 26.89)/3.361 = 12.19, \\
x_3:\quad F &= (28.33 - 26.89)/3.361 = 0.43 .
\end{align*}
The smallest, $0.43 < 4$, so $x_3$ leaves. In the model $x_1, x_2$ ($MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 3.148$), deleting $x_1$
gives $F = 47.0$ and deleting $x_2$ gives $F = 16.96$, both above $4$. Stop. The model is again $x_1, x_2$.
Here all methods agree; Problem~\ref{prob:fwd-bwd} shows that they need not.
\end{solution}

\subsection{Cautions in model selection}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \emph{There is no single best model.} Different criteria, and different search methods, can
choose different models, and several models are often nearly equally good. Variable selection
should produce a short list of candidates, to be judged by residual analysis, by subject-matter
knowledge, and by how sensible the coefficients are.
\item \emph{Stepwise methods do not search every model.} Forward selection never removes a
regressor; backward elimination never brings one back. When regressors are correlated, a variable
that looks best alone may be useless in combination, and the two methods can end in different
places, neither of them the best subset (Problem~\ref{prob:fwd-bwd}).
\item \emph{Inference after selection is optimistic.} The $t$ and $F$ statistics of the chosen model
are computed as if it had been specified in advance. It was not: it was chosen because its
statistics looked good. Its $P$-values are too small, its $R^2$ too large and its residual mean
square too small. Where possible, a model chosen on one set of data should be checked on fresh
data, or on a part of the data held back for the purpose (\emph{validation}).
\item \emph{Respect the structure of the model.} If $x^2$ or an interaction $x_1x_2$ is kept, the
lower-order terms $x$, $x_1$ and $x_2$ are normally kept too, whatever their individual $t$ values, so
that the model does not depend on the arbitrary origin of the measurement scale.
\item \emph{Multicollinearity makes selection unstable.} When regressors are strongly correlated, small
changes in the data can change which of them is chosen. This is the subject of the next section.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Practice problems}
\begin{problem}\label{prob:cp-full}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Show that for the full model, $C_p = p$ exactly.
\item Show that $C_p$ can be negative, and say what a negative value suggests.
\item Show that maximising $R^2_{\mathrm{adj}}$ over all models is the same as minimising $MS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) For the full model $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = (n - p)\hat\sigma^2$, so $C_p = (n - p) - n + 2p = p$.

(b) $C_p < 0$ when $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)/\hat\sigma^2 < n - 2p$, that is,
$MS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p) < \hat\sigma^2(n - 2p)/(n - p)$, which is possible since $MS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)$ is a random
variable that can fall below $\hat\sigma^2$. Since $\Gamma_p \ge p > 0$, a negative $C_p$ is pure sampling
error, and usually means that the subset model is unbiased and its residual mean square happened to
be small; it can also mean that $\hat\sigma^2$ is inflated by superfluous terms in the full model.

(c) $R^2_{\mathrm{adj},p} = 1 - MS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)\big/[SS_T/(n - 1)]$, and $SS_T/(n - 1)$ is the same for
every model fitted to the same responses. So $R^2_{\mathrm{adj}}$ is a decreasing function of
$MS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)$.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:omitted}
Suppose the true model is $y = \beta_0 + \beta_1x_1 + \beta_2x_2 + \varepsilon$ but $y$ is regressed on
$x_1$ alone.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Show that $E(\hat\beta_1^*) = \beta_1 + \beta_2 S_{12}/S_{11}$, where
$S_{11} = \sum(x_{i1} - \bar x_1)^2$ and $S_{12} = \sum(x_{i1} - \bar x_1)x_{i2}$.
\item When is the slope estimate unbiased despite the omission?
\item A study finds that households with more radios have more children at school, and concludes that
radios cause school attendance. Explain the likely error in terms of (a).
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) By Theorem~\ref{thm:under-over}(i), $E(\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}_1^*) = \boldsymbol\beta_1 + \mathbf{A}\beta_2$, where
$\mathbf{A}$ holds the coefficients of the simple regression of $x_2$ on $x_1$. Its slope is $S_{12}/S_{11}$.
(Directly: $\hat\beta_1^* = \sum(x_{i1} - \bar x_1)y_i/S_{11}$, and substituting
$E(y_i) = \beta_0 + \beta_1x_{i1} + \beta_2x_{i2}$ gives $\beta_1 + \beta_2S_{12}/S_{11}$, since
$\sum(x_{i1} - \bar x_1) = 0$.)

(b) When $\beta_2 = 0$, or when $S_{12} = 0$, so that $x_1$ and $x_2$ are uncorrelated in the sample.
This is one reason for designing experiments with uncorrelated (orthogonal) factors.

(c) An omitted variable, such as household income, raises both the number of radios and school
attendance. In the regression of attendance on radios alone, the radio coefficient is
$\beta_1 + \beta_2S_{12}/S_{11}$, where $\beta_2$ is the effect of income and $S_{12}/S_{11}$ the slope of
income on radios. Even if radios have no effect ($\beta_1 = 0$), the estimate is positive.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:aic-bic}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Show that, in comparing a model with $p$ parameters to one with $p + 1$, AIC prefers the larger
model exactly when $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)/SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p + 1) > e^{2/n}$, and find the corresponding condition
for BIC.
\item For $n = 12$, express each condition as a partial $F$ statistic, and compare with $F_{\mathrm{in}} = 4$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) $\mathrm{AIC}_{p+1} < \mathrm{AIC}_p$ means $n\ln SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p + 1) + 2(p + 1) < n\ln SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p) + 2p$
(the $-n\ln n$ terms cancel), that is, $\ln[SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)/SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p + 1)] > 2/n$. For BIC the penalty
difference is $\ln n$ instead of $2$, so the condition is $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)/SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p + 1) > n^{1/n}$.

(b) The partial $F$ statistic for the added term is
$F = (n - p - 1)\bigl[SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p)/SS_{\mathrm{Res}}(p + 1) - 1\bigr]$. With $n = 12$ and, say, $p = 2$ (so
$n - p - 1 = 9$):
\begin{align*}
\text{AIC:}\quad F &> 9\,(e^{2/12} - 1) = 9 \times 0.1814 = 1.63, \\
\text{BIC:}\quad F &> 9\,(12^{1/12} - 1) = 9 \times 0.2300 = 2.07 .
\end{align*}
Both are more lenient than $F_{\mathrm{in}} = 4$, and AIC more than BIC. With small samples, AIC in
particular tends to admit terms that a significance test would reject.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:fwd-bwd}
Ten observations on three candidate regressors gave the following residual sums of squares:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l|r||l|r}
Regressors & $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}$ & Regressors & $SS_{\mathrm{Res}}$ \\
\hline
none & 474.5 & $x_1, x_2$ & 32.56 \\
$x_1$ & 39.97 & $x_1, x_3$ & 33.59 \\
$x_2$ & 82.66 & $x_2, x_3$ & 3.42 \\
$x_3$ & 178.09 & $x_1, x_2, x_3$ & 3.40 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
In these data $x_1$ is roughly the average of $x_2$ and $x_3$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item Carry out forward selection and backward elimination with $F_{\mathrm{in}} = F_{\mathrm{out}} = 4$.
\item Which model do $C_p$ and BIC choose?
\item Explain the difference.
\end{enumerate}
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) \emph{Forward.} Step 1: the single-regressor $F$ values are
$(474.5 - 39.97)/(39.97/8) = 87.0$, $(474.5 - 82.66)/(82.66/8) = 37.9$ and
$(474.5 - 178.09)/(178.09/8) = 13.3$; $x_1$ enters. Step 2: given $x_1$, adding $x_2$ gives
$F = (39.97 - 32.56)/(32.56/7) = 1.59$, and adding $x_3$ gives $(39.97 - 33.59)/(33.59/7) = 1.33$. Both
are below $4$, so forward selection stops with $x_1$ alone, $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 39.97$.

\emph{Backward.} In the full model $MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 3.40/6 = 0.566$. Deleting $x_1$ gives
$F = (3.42 - 3.40)/0.566 = 0.05$; deleting $x_2$, $(33.59 - 3.40)/0.566 = 53.3$; deleting $x_3$,
$(32.56 - 3.40)/0.566 = 51.5$. Remove $x_1$. In the model $x_2, x_3$ ($MS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 3.42/7 = 0.489$),
deleting $x_2$ gives $F = (178.09 - 3.42)/0.489 = 357$ and deleting $x_3$ gives
$(82.66 - 3.42)/0.489 = 162$. Stop with $x_2, x_3$, $SS_{\mathrm{Res}} = 3.42$.

(b) With $\hat\sigma^2 = 0.566$ and $n = 10$:
$C_p(x_1) = 39.97/0.566 - 10 + 4 = 64.6$, $C_p(x_2x_3) = 3.42/0.566 - 10 + 6 = 2.04$, and every other
model with $p < 4$ has $C_p > 50$. BIC is likewise smallest for $x_2, x_3$
($10\ln 0.342 + 3\ln 10 = -3.81$, against $18.5$ for $x_1$). Both choose $x_2, x_3$.

(c) Because $x_1$ is close to the average of $x_2$ and $x_3$, it is the best single stand-in for both,
and forward selection, which looks one regressor at a time, takes it first. Once $x_1$ is in, $x_2$
and $x_3$ each add little separately, since $x_1$ already carries most of what each contributes,
so neither enters. But $y$ depends on $x_2$ and $x_3$ with \emph{different} coefficients, which $x_1$
cannot mimic, and the pair together fits ten times better. Backward elimination starts from the
model that contains the pair and finds that $x_1$ is the redundant one. When regressors are
correlated, one-at-a-time searches can miss the best subset; this is the strongest argument for
examining all subsets when it is feasible.
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}\label{prob:farms-press}
For the farm data, the model in $x_1, x_2$ has $R^2 = 0.951$ and $\mathrm{PRESS} = 46.28$, and the
full model has $R^2 = 0.953$ and $\mathrm{PRESS} = 68.74$. Compute $R^2_{\mathrm{pred}}$ for both and comment.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
$R^2_{\mathrm{pred}} = 1 - \mathrm{PRESS}/SS_T$ with $SS_T = 574.67$: for $x_1, x_2$,
$1 - 46.28/574.67 = 0.919$; for the full model, $1 - 68.74/574.67 = 0.880$. The full model fits the
data slightly better but predicts new farms worse. The extra term has adapted to noise in these twelve
farms, and that noise does not recur. The gap between $R^2$ and $R^2_{\mathrm{pred}}$ is $0.032$ for the
smaller model and $0.073$ for the larger, a typical symptom of overfitting.
\end{solution}
